So max of the six {3x,3y,3z,s,s,s} is 3x, attained ≥ 2 ⟹ sum = {c ≤ 3x} ∋ −∞ ✓. And if all x=y=z=−∞: P = {−∞} ∋ 0 ✓. ✓ Tropical confirmed.

OK so the inclusion idea fails. Back to the witness/intersection idea, or another approach.

Let me reconsider. We want 0 ∈ P. Equivalent formulations:

So in "genuinely hyper" fields (sign, tropical, phase), a ∈ a⊞a⊞a holds! In fields it fails. Interesting. Is a ∈ a⊞a⊞a a THEOREM in hyperfields that are not fields? Hyperfield H = ℝ/G, G = {±2ⁿ}. Nonzero elements: sign × magnitude-class, magnitude classes = ℝ_{>0}/2^ℤ. Let me denote elements by pairs (ε, m) with ε ∈ {±1}, m ∈ ℝ_{>0}/2^ℤ (i.e., real > 0 mod doubling), plus 0.

Addition: a⊞b = closure... quotient hyperfield addition: π(a) ⊞ π(b) = {π(a+b) : ...} = {π(u+v) : u ∈ π^{-1}(a), v ∈ π^{-1}(b)}. Since π^{-1}(ε, m) = {ε m 2^n}.

Compute (1, m)⊞(1, m) = {π(2m 2^n)} = {(1, 2m)} = {(1,m)}! Because 2m ≡ m mod 2^ℤ!! Oh nice: 2m and m are in the same magnitude class (multiply by 2 ∈ G). So (1,m)⊞(1,m) = {(1,m)} ✓ idempotent, like tropical. Similarly (−1,m)⊞(−1,m) = {(−1,m)}.

(1,m)⊞(1,m') with m < m' (WLOG in the order... magnitude classes mod 2^ℤ: each class has a representative in [1,2)): {π(m 2^n + m' 2^k)}: sums of positive numbers: m2^n + m'2^k = 2^k(m 2^{n−k} + m'): the class of (m 2^{n−k} + m'): with t = 2^{n−k} varying over all powers of 2: classes of {mt + m' : t ∈ 2^ℤ}. mt ranges over class m; so mt + m' ranges over an interval... e.g., m=1, m'=1 (same class): computed above = class of 2 = class 1. m=1, m' = 1.5: {t + 1.5 : t ∈ 2^ℤ} = {..., 0.25+1.5=1.75, 0.5+1.5=2, 1+1.5=2.5, 2+1.5=3.5, ...}: classes: 1.75, 1 (=2), 1.25 (=2.5/2), 1.75 (=3.5/2), ... so {(1,1.75), (1,1), (1,1.25)}: hmm interesting, a set of 3 elements. So this hyperfield is quite rich.

(1,m)⊞(−1,m') (opposite signs): {π(m 2^n − m' 2^k)}: can be zero (if m2^n = m'2^k, i.e., same class m = m'), and otherwise: differences mt − m' with t ∈ 2^ℤ: both signs occur, various magnitudes: if m = m' (same class): {0} ∪ {±(|mt − m'|)}: e.g., m = m' = 1: {t − 1 : t ∈ 2^ℤ} = {±1, ±3, ±7, ±15...}... classes: 1, 3, 7, 15 → each class: 1, 1.5, 1.75, 1.875...: so {(±1, 1), (±1,1.5), (±1,1.75), ...} ∪ {0}: infinitely many elements.
